By Place of Supply

With effect from | January 2020, new rules were introduced in relation to call-off stock and consignment
stock arrangements,

3.1 Consignment and call-off stock

Irish VAT legislation provides for a simplification measure for call-off stock arrangements.

The relief applies only to EU VAT registered taxable persons. Where the non-established taxable person
makes an intra-Community acquisition of goods in Ireland (i.e. the goods are dispatched from one Member
State to Ireland under a pre-existing agreement between the supplier and the intended purchaser) and
subsequently supplies those goods to an accountable person in Ireland (providing the conditions are met), the
intra-Community acquisition and the following supply by the EU taxable person must be disregarded and the
customer must account for the intra-Community acquisition,

The accountable person in Ireland accounts for VAT on the goods at the time that ownership of the goods
transfers,

The legislative conditions require that:

+ the supplier must not be established in Ireland nor have a fixed establishment in Ireland;

+ the purchaser is VAT registered in Ireland (the identity and VAT number of the purchaser is known to the
supplier at the time of dispatch);

+ the goods are dispatched under a pre-existing agreement between the supplier and the purchaser; and

+ the supplier must keep a record in relation to all transactions covered by the simplification measure.

‘The supplier should hold the stock in an independent warehouse to avoid any risk of being viewed as having
an establishment in Ireland for VAT purposes.

The above simplification measure applies where ownership of the goods only transfers to the purchaser at a
time after arrival of the goods in Ireland but within 12 months of that arrival. The measure is only applicable
to intra~Community acquisitions, not imports.

Simplification measures apply to consignment stock which describes the movernent of stock by a supplier
in one Member State to Ireland to be held as stock in Ireland and is provided to purchasers when stock is
required. The consignment stock must remain under the control of the supplier and must not be intended for
any one known purchaser at the time of transport.

The legislative conditions require that:

+ the EU supplier must not be established in Ireland;

+ the EU supplier has not exercised an option to register for VAT in Ireland;

+ the BU supplier is not otherwise required to be VAT registered in Ireland; and
+ the purchaser is VAT registered in Ireland.

Ireland | 297

--- Page 114 ---
3.2. Installation supplies

Goods that are installed or assembled in Ireland, with or without a trial run, by or on behalf of the supplier

are supplied in Ireland for VAT purposes. There is no formal definition of what constitutes installation or
assembly, although Tax Briefing 58 provides guidance on the Irish Revenue interpretation of “install” and
“assemble” which follows the VAT Committee guidelines. “Install” is normally taken to mean the placing in
position of equipment or machinery. “Assemble” is laken to mean the fitting or gathering together of parts

of equipment or of a machine. Generally, the terms are often referred to by whether the good constitutes
“fixture” or a “fitting”. A fixture is an item that cannot be easily removed without causing damage to the good
or its surroundings and is taxed as a supply of goods. A “fitting” may be easily removed and is generally taxed
asa supply of a service.

A reverse charge provision applies to supplies of goods that are installed in Ireland, with or without a trial run,
by a non-established supplier where the customer is a taxable person who carries on a business in Ireland, or
a public body. In any other case the non-established supplier would be obliged to register for and charge Irish
VAT on the supply, such as where the customer is a private individual,

If the non-established supplicr is supplying services related to fixtures, it would be obliged to register for
Irish VAT and account for VAT on the service supplied. The exception to this rule would be if the services
fall within the scope of a reverse charge that applies for services performed under a “relevant contract”
for Relevant Contracts Tax. If these services fall within the scope of Relevant Contracts Tax, they must be
separately considered because a reverse charge may apply. This is a complex area of tax law.

33 Distance sales threshold

The distance sales threshold is currently EUR 10,000, A non-established taxable person may elect to VAT
register in Ireland at any time prior to its exceeding the threshold. No additional administrative requirements
apply. Once registered, the non-established taxable person is treated in the same way as any other VAT
registered person,

34 Simplified triangulation rules

The simplified triangulation rules have been implemented in Ireland in line with the EU VAT Directive.

Where Lreland is the final State of destination of the goods, the wording of the Irish legislation does not

preclude the application of the triangulation rules if the middleman is VAT registered in Ireland or in the
country of dispatch.

There are no simplification measures applicable to chain supplies. The circumstances of each transaction
should be examtined to ascertain if some of the transactions within a chain supply fall within the triangulation
simplification measures.

3.5 Acquisition threshold

The threshold for the acquisition of goods by non-taxable legal persons, taxable persons engaged in exempt
activities and flat-rate farmers from suppliers established in another EU Member State is EUR 41,000, Where
acquisitions of goods will exceed, or are likely to exceed, this threshold in any continuous 12-month period,
VAT must be accounted for in respect of such acquisitions.

New means of transport and excisable products are excluded from this threshold and are always taxed in the
State of destination (i.e. no threshold applies).

3.6 Effective use and enjoyment

Irish VAT legislation contains a number of use and enjoyment provisions on the basis of Article 59a(b)
Directive:

+ hiring of movable goods that are supplied outside the EU but where the services are used and enjoyed in
Iretand - place of supply is Ireland (applies to supplies made to taxable and non-taxable persons);

298 | Ireland

--- Page 115 ---
+ supply to a non-taxable person of telecommunications, radio, broadcasting services or telephone cards
that are supplied outside the EU but which are used and enjoyed in Ireland - place of supply is Ireland;

+ supply to a non-taxable person of financial services (excluding safe deposit facilities) and insurance
(including reinsurance) which are supplied outside the EU but are used and enjoyed in Ireland - place of
supply is Ireland; and

+ where money transfer services that are provided to a person in Ireland are used and enjoyed in Ireland, the
place of supply of related intermediary services to a principal outside the EU will be treated as supplied in
Ireland.

Ireland introduced the following use & enjoyment provision on the basis of Article 59a(a) VAT Directive:

+ hiring of a means of transport that is supplied in Ireland but where the services are used and enjoyed
outside the EU - place of supply is outside the EU (applies to supplies made to taxable and non-taxable
persons).

4. Chargeable event & Chargeability to VAT

4.1 Principles

‘The general principle is that in the case of a supply of goods, the time of supply is when the actual transfer
of ownership of the goods by agreement occurs (when the goods are supplied). In the case of a supply of
services, the time of supply is when the services are supplied.

However, where an invoice is required to be issued, then the VAT is due on the date that the invoice is issued,
or if not issued on time, the latest date by which it should have issued. The latest date for issuing an invoice is
the 15" of the month following the month in which the supply occurred.

4.2 Advance payments

In situations where a payment is received in advance of the supply being made, then the time of supply
for VAT purposes is the time the payment is received. An invoice must be issued in respect of the advance
payment by the 15" of the month following the month in which the payment was received.

4.3 Continuous supplies

‘The VAT Directive provides that continuous supplies of goods and services over a period of time are regarded
as being completed at least at intervals of one year and under Irish VAT law, this rule applies in relation to
supplies of telecommunications services, gas and electricity (where such supplies are made to unregistered
customers, the VAT becomes due when a bill is issued provided it is issued at least once every 3 months). The
normal rules apply in all other cases.

44 Cash accounting

Traders can account for VAT on the cash receipts basis provided certain conditions are met; the total turnover
from supplies is less than EUR 2 million or less than 90% of turnover relates to supplies to unregistered
persons or persons who are not entitled to claim a full VAT deduction. The cash receipts basis does not apply
in the case of supplies between connected persons, in respect of imports or in respect of intra-Community
acquisitions.

5. Taxable amount

5.1 Bad Debt Relief

Ireland has provisions for bad debt relief for accountable persons that operate the “invoice basis” of
accounting for VAT and that meet the conditions set out in legislation (the relief is not applicable to taxable
persons that operate the “cash receipts” basis of VAT accounting).

Ireland | 299

--- Page 116 ---
‘The obligations to be satisfied by the accountable person seeking to claim the relief include that:

+ they have taken all reasonable steps to recover the bad debt;
+ the debt is allowable as a deduction for Corporation Tax or Income Tax purposes;

+ the bad debt has been written off in the financial accounts (it cannot be a “doubtful debt”):

+ the person from whom the debt is due is unconnected to the accountable person;

+ ifpartial payment has been received the relief must be pro-rated; and

+ if the debt is written off and (partial) payment is received, the relief claimed must be repaid.

The claim is made through the VAT3 retum by increasing the input VAT figure in the T2 box of the return,
An adjustment is made if payment is subsequently received + his is also done through the VAT3 return by
reducing the figure in the T2 box of the return. There is no specific time limit for making a claim for bad debt
relief but the 4-year rule applicable to refunds must be taken into account.

There is an obligation to keep relevant records which could be examined in the event of an Irish Revenue
audit, The records must contain the following information:

* name and address of the debtor;
+ nature of the goods and services;
© date debt incurred;

+ date debt written off;

+ copy VAT invoice; and
«record of VAT accounted for.

Objective evidence of steps taken to recover the bad debt must be retained.

5.2 Cash discounts

Assume that the supplier sends the following invoice:

“Supply of goods EUR 1,000
23% VAT EUR _230
Total to be paid EUR 1,230

If payment is received by us within the next 14 days, you can subtract 2% of the invoice as a cash discount.”

Payment is indeed received within 14 days. In that case the supplier has to pay EUR 230 in its VAT return.
The customer can deduct EUR 230 in its VAT return. Therefore, the supplier has paid too much VAT and

the customer has deducted too much. If the supplier is accounting for VAT on the moneys received basis,

the supplier must issue a credit note showing VAT where the discount is allowed following the issue of the
invoice. A credit note must also be issued in the case of a supplier who accounts for VAT on the invoice basis.
However, where the supplier operates the invoice basis and both parties agree in writing not to make any
change to the VAT shown on the original invoice, there is no need to issue a credit note in respect of the VAT.

5.3 Open Market Value

Open market value can be imposed by Irish Revenue on supplies of goods and services between connected
parties in order to ensure the correct collection of VAT.

Irish Revenue may impose market value if they are satisfied that the actual consideration in relation to the
supply between the connected parties is either higher or lower than open market value.

In order to be ‘connected’ for the application of the open market value rule, the supplier and the recipient

of the goods and services must be connected by financial or legal ties, being party to any agreement,
undertaking, promise or understanding (whether express or implied and whether legally enforceable or not)
or either the supplier or the recipient (of the supply of goods or services) must exercise control over the other.
‘Control’ for these purposes means the power to control the affairs of the other party.

300 | Ireland

--- Page 117 ---
»  ifactual consideration is lower than open market value, market value can be imposed in instances where:
+ he recipient of the supply must have less than 100% VAT recovery entitlement on the supply or must
bea flat-rate farmer; or
+ the supplier is making a VAT exempt supply and the supplier is engaged in any non-deductible
business activities or is a “flat-rate” farmer.

+ If the actual consideration is higher than open market value, for market value to be applied, the supplier
must be a flat-rate farmer or must be engaged in non-deductible business activities.

5.4 Returnable packaging

The Irish Deposit Return Scheme (the “DRS”) came into operation in Ireland on 1 February 2024, The
DRS requires that a deposit (of 15c or 25c) is charged when drink products are supplied in certain single-
use containers (cans and plastic bottles), and the deposit is later refunded to a person who returns the empty
container for recycling/reuse. The refundable deposit is charged by relevant suppliers at each stage of the
supply chain in the State: manufacturer or importer, wholesalers, retailers, etc. Under the DRS, the deposit
is added to the price of the goods by the person who first supplies the drink product in the State (i., the
manufacturer or the importer), and by any subsequent supplier (e.g. a wholesaler, distributer, retailer). Once
the drink has been consumed, if the empty container is returned under the Scheme, then the deposit will be
refunded.

Businesses supplying DRS products have no VAT liability relating to the deposit. A VAT liability arises on the
deposits relating to containers that are not returned to the DRS. Under VAT law, only the Scheme Operator
must account for and pay VAT relating to deposits on unreturned containers. This liability does not affect the
consumer or any of the businesses in the supply chain for the product.

6. VAT rates and exemptions

61 Standard rate

‘The standard rate of VAT in Ireland is currently 23%. A temporary rate of 21% applied in the period 1
September 2020 to 28 February 2021.

6.2 Reduced rate

Reduced rates in Ireland are as follows.
A reduced rate of 13.5% applies to the following types of goods and services in Ireland:

+ certain agricultural goods and services;

+ waste collection and recycling services;

+ VATable supplies of immovable property;

* supplies of coal, peat and other solid fuels, electricity and gas;

+ supplies of certain photographic and related supplies;

+ — short-term hiring of cars, pleasure boats, caravans and mobile homes;

+ repair and maintenance of movable goods;

+ services consisting of the care of the human body, including services supplied in the course of a health
studio business or similar business;

* — veterinary services;

+ non-oral contraceptive products;

+ children’s car safety seats;

« hot drinks and hot takeaway food;

+ vending machine sales;

+ meals in cafes and restaurants:

+ hotel rooms, guesthouses, caravan parks, campsites;

Ireland | 301

--- Page 118 ---
+ admission to cinemas, theatres, certain musical performances, exhibitions, museums, cultural, artistic or
scientific events, amusement park services;

+ certain printed matter, inchiding newspapers, brochures, leaflets, catalogues, programs, maps (but
excluding books or advertising material);

* membership or admission to commercial sports facilities;

+ commercial golf club membership, green fees, driving range fees and incidental equipment hire; and

+ hairdressing and beautician services.

The second reduced rate of 9% now applies to the following types of goods and services in Iretand:

+ periodicals;

+ e-periodicals (excluding those wholly or predominantly devoted to advertising or consisting of audio or
video content); and

* the provision by a person of facilities for sporting purposes (other than for a profit making organisation).

There is a further reduced rate of 4.8% that applies to supplies of livestock and a flat rate compensation
percentage for farmers of 5.1% (with effect from 1 January 2025).

7. Exemptions

7.1 Exemptions with right to deduction (zero rate)

The zero rate of Irish VAT may be applied to supplies of goods and services including:

+ exports and intra-Community supplies of goods;

+ — imports of goods that are consigned to another EU Member State;

+ services relating to vessels and aircraft including the supply, modification, repair, maintenance, chartering
and hiring of seagoing vessels, related equipment and supplies for the fuelling and provisioning of certain
vessels and aircraft;

+ certain intermediary services;

+ certain supplies of food and drink;

+ certain supplies of printed matter;

+ children’s clothing and footwear;

+ supplies of oral medicine for humans and certain animals, and the supply of certain medical equipment;

+ — supply of audiobooks and e-books;

+ supply and installation of solar panels on school buildings;

+ supplies of fertilisers, feeding stuff and certain seeds;

* personal protection equipment (PPE);

* thermometers;

+ hand sanitiser; medical ventilators and specialist respiratory equipment such as respirators for intensive
and sub-intensive care and other oxygen therapy apparatus including oxygen tents and oxygen in certain
specified circumstances.

In relation to the zero rating of goods that are to be exported or dispatched out of Ireland, the supplier should

retain proof of transport of the goods out of the State. For intra~-Community dispatches, the supplier is obliged

to confirm that the customer has a valid VAT number and retain proof that the goods left Ireland.

7.1.1 Warehousing

7.1.1.1 VAT warehouse
Ireland does not currently operate a dedicated VAT warehouse regime.

The supply of alcohol products within an approved excise tax warehouse is disregarded for Irish VAT
purposes. The importation or intra-Community acquisition of alcoho! goods into an excise tax warehouse is
similarly disregarded for VAT purposes.

7.1.1.2 Bonded (customs) warehouse

Treland operates a customs warehousing regime under which imports into an approved warehouse delays

302 | Ireland

--- Page 119 ---
the import VAT and customs liability until the goods are released out of the warehouse for the EU market.
‘Warehouses can be public, where they are used to store goods on behalf of any accountable person, or
private warehouses, which are reserved for use by authorised accountable persons. Within the public’private
categories there are six different types of warehouses in Ireland, A to F. There is no limit on the time that
goods may be stored in these warehouses.

Goods that are bought and sold within an Irish customs warehouse are subject to VAT.

7.2 Exemptions without right to deduction

Schedule | of the VAT Consolidation Act 2010 sets out the activities which are exempt from VAT. Examples
of such exempt activities are:

+ public postal services;

* certain medical and related services;

+ professional dental or optical services;

+ the provision by a recognised body (as specifically defined) of children’s or young people’s education,
school or university education, or vocational training or retraining (including the supply of goods and
services incidental to that provision, other than the supply of research services);

+ catering services supplied to patients of a hospital or nursing home in the hospital or nursing home, or to
students at their school;

+ the provision of national broadcasting and television services, excluding advertising;

+ certain financial and insurance services;

+ the provision of services by a funeral undertaking;

+ transporting passengers and their accompanying baggage; and

* certain property transactions.

7.3 Options for taxation

An option to tax applies only to the following exempt activities:

+ supplies of investment gold (a waiver of exemption can be applied for);

+ letting of immovable property (which does not constitute a freehold equivalent interest); and

+ sale of exempt immovable property (this must be done by way of a joint option to tax in writing by both
parties. It is usually evidenced in the contract for sale), Both parties exercising the joint option to tax are
required to be taxable persons and carrying on a business in the State.

8. Liability

8.1 Reverse charge

There are certain instances where the customer of the supply of goods or services is required to account for
VAT on the reverse charge basis rather than the supplier charging VAT to the customer.

Under Irish legislation, cross-border services supplied to a person that is engaged in an economic activity (ic.
is a taxable person) will be subject to a reverse charge (unless the exceptions to this general rule apply). Ifthe
recipient is not engaged in an economic activity, the service will be supplied where the supplier is established.
A clear reference to the application of the reverse charge should be noted on the invoice issued. This reverse
charge applies in all EU Member States and follows from Articles 44 and 196 EU VAT Directive. In addition
there is also the reverse charge for the supply of natural gas and electricity supplied by non-resident suppliers
to “taxable dealers” in Ireland.

For services which are connected with immovable property supplied by non-established suppliers, a reverse
charge obligation arises for established taxable persons or public bodies except where the services are (i) a

provision of accommodation in a hotel or guesthouse or in an establishment having a similar function, or in
a holiday camp or a site developed for use as a camping site, (ii) the supply of telecommunications services,

Ireland | 303

--- Page 120 ---
radio or television broadcasting services or electronically supplied services, together with the provision
of accommodation of the kind specified in (i), where the supply is by the provider of that accommodation
acting in his or her own name, and (iii) a supply of services involving the preparation and co-ordination
of construction work (including a supply of services of architects and of persons who provide on-site
supervision).

8.1.1 General reverse charge mechanism - non-resident suppliers (Article 194 EU VAT Directive)
Ireland did not implement a reverse charge for the domestic supply of goods by non-resident suppliers, other
than for installation supplies. There is a reverse charge for non-resident suppliers that supply goods which
require installation provided the customer in Ireland is a taxable person or a public body established in
Ireland.

8.1.2 Reverse charge mechanism - specific domestic rules (Article 199 EU VAT Directive)
Ireland also has local reverse charges for certain types of supplies including the following:

+ certain supplies of immovable goods to the National Asset Management Agency;

+ supplies of greenhouse gas emissions between taxable persons that conduct businesses in Ireland;

+ supplies of construction services by a subcontractor to a principal which are within the scope of Relevant
Contracts Tax. The subcontractor can be established outside of Ireland for the reverse charge to apply.
If the overseas supplier is the principal in receipt of the construction services then it will be obliged to
register for Irish VAT in order to operate the reverse charge on the scrvices received, whether supplied by
an Irish or an overseas subcontractor;

+ supplies of scrap metal between taxable persons that conduct businesses in Ireland; and

+ supplies of construction services and engineering works between connected persons.

The application of these reverse charges is mandatory where the conditions are satisfied.

The law in relation to these reverse charges, in particular any related to property, are complex and the
legislation should be reviewed in detail.

8.2 di nt and several liability

In the context of VAT groups, cach member of an Irish VAT group is jointly and severally liable tor the VAT
liabilities of the group under Irish VAT law.

‘There is also an anti-avoidance provision in Irish law which provides that where VAT bas been fraudulently
evaded, an accountable person who knowingly or recklessly participated in transactions connected to that
fraudulent evasion is jointly and severally liable for the VAT that has not been accounted for. In other words,
a recipient of a supply may be jointly and severally liable with the supplier for VAT liabilities where they
knew or were reckless as to whether the supplier was engaged in evasion. Where particular goods or services
pass through a chain of transactions involving a number of persons, any or all of those persons who knew

or were reckless as to whether or not there was fraudulent evasion of VAT in relation to the supply of those
goods and/or services, may be assessed by Irish Revenue for the VAT arising on the basis that they are jointly
and severally liable for the unpaid VAT.

9: Recovery of input VAT

91 Non-recoverable input VAT
In Ireland, input VAT is not deductible on the following items:

+ food, drink, accommodation (other than for a qualifying conference) or any other personal services for the
accountable person, his agents or employees, except to the extent, if any, that such provision constitutes a
supply of services in respect of which the accountable person is accountable for VAT;

+ expenditure on the acquisition or development of immovable goods for any purpose other than the
accountable person’s business;

+ expenditure incurred by an accountable person on food or drink, or accommodation or other entertainment

304 | Ireland

--- Page 121 ---
services, where such expenditure forms all or part of the cost of providing an advertising service in
respect of which tax is due and payable by the accountable person;

+ entertainment expenses incurred by the accountable person, their agents or their employees;

+ purchase, hiring, intra-Community acquisition or importation of motor vehicles, other than as stock- in-
trade, a car hire business or use in a driving school business (although a 20% deduction may be claimed
for “qualifying vehicles” where strict conditions are satisfied);

+ purchase, intra~Community acquisition or importation of petrol, other than as stock-in-trade;

+ goods purchased by an accountable person which are subject to a VAT margin scheme; and

+ contract work involving the handing over of goods when such goods are themselves not deductible.

9.2 Excess input VAT

Excess input VAT is refunded by Irish Revenue, The excess input VAT will be refunded following the
submission of each VAT return, but the tax authorities may seek to review supporting documentation for any
claim.

93 Capital goods - adjustment periods

The adjustment period for immovable goods that are subject to the capital goods scheme are as follows:

= capital goods - 20 intervals from the date of completion; and
+ refurbishment capital goods - 10 intervals from the date of completion of the refurbishment.

An “interval” means either an initial interval, a second interval or a “subsequent interval”. An initial interval
is a period of 12 months from the date the capital good is completed or acquired. A second interval begins

on the day following the end of the initial interval for that capital good and ends on the final day of the
accounting year during which the second interval begins. Subsequent intervals mean each accounting year
of a capital goods owner in the adjustment period applicable to that capital good, which follows the second
interval, A refurbishment capital good is created when there is development on a previously completed capital
good.

A previously completed capital good is one which has reached a point of “completion” (per the definition of
“completion” in Irish VAT legislation) and further development work has been undertaken, such further work
creating another separate capital good which is referred to as a refurbishment capital good.

‘The capital goods scheme in Ireland does not extend to movable property or services.

94 VAT refund to EU taxable persons

Since | January 2010 VAT refund claims by non-resident taxable persons must be submitted through the
online portal of the tax authority in which the taxable person is established. For Irish accountable persons
who incur VAT in another EU Member State, the claim must be submitted online via ROS (Revenue-On-Line
System) and the relevant section is called EVR (Electronic VAT Refund).

When an application is submitted online, a message is forwarded to the applicant's ROS inbox stating

that their application has been received by Irish Revenue and that it has been forwarded to the relevant

EU Member State for refund. If an application is successful, a decision message will be forwarded to the
applicant’s ROS inbox. Payment will then be made (by electronic fund transfer) to the bank account provided
in the application. Invoices and importation documents are generally not required but can be forwarded with
the application as a scared attachment. These attachments must not exceed SMB in size. Irish Revenue may
also request additional information before a final decision is made on an application. The applicant is notified
directly via the contact details provided in its application i.e. e-mail or telephone. Irish Revenue must issue a
decision on the refund claim within 4 months of receipt of the claim, This period can be extended to 6 months
or 8 months in cases where Irish Revenue requests additional information.

‘The annual minimum limit for claims that are made for a calendar year or the remainder of a calendar year
is EUR 50. The interim minimum limit for claims that are made for less than a calendar year but at least 3
months is EUR 400.

Ireland | 305

--- Page 122 ---
‘The minimum period for making a claim is 3 months within a calendar year (or less if it starts after | October)
and the maximum claim period is onc ycar. The deadline for submitting the claim is 30 September of the year
following the calendar year in which the VAT was incurred. This is a strict deadline.

ROS allows for the submission of a maximum of 5 applications to each EU Member State in a calendar year.
In the event that the claimant is not successful, where Irish Revenue either refuuse the claim in full or in part,
the decision by Irish Revenue can be appealed. The time limit for submitting an appeal is within 30 days after
the notification.

‘The same rules which apply to domestic taxable persons as regards the non-deductible items, equally apply to
non-established taxable persons reclaiming Irish VAT. The non-deductible items are listed above at 9.1.

95 VAT refund to non-EU taxable persons

The electronic refuund system which applies to EU taxable persons does not apply in the case of non-EU taxable
persons. A paper application form must be completed which is called a VAT60OEC and is available on the Irish
Revenue website (see link below). The rules apply to claimants conducting business outside the FU. Ireland
provides reciprocity where (he country of the claimant has a similar scheme for refunding to claimants in Ireland,

The application must be accompanied by original invoices showing the amount of VAT paid. If the non-EU
taxable person wants the original invoices returned to him, a set of photocopies should be included with the
application. Invoices should contain the following particulars:

+ the supplier's name, address and VAT registration number;

+ the name and address of the person to whom the goods or services were supplied;
+ the date of issue of the invoice;

+ asequential number which uniquely identifies the invoice;

+ adetailed description of the goods and services supplied, and

+ the total cost, the rate of VAT and the VAT charged.

The application must be accompanied by a certificate issued by the relevant authority of the country in which
the claimant is established stating the economic activity in which the claimant is engaged and providing the
following information:

+ the name, address and official stamp of the authority which issued same;

+ the name and address of the claimant; and

+ the claimant’s VAT registration number and a statement as to the nature of the business conducted by the
claimant.

However, if the claimant has already forwarded such a certificate to Irish Revenue, it is not necessary to
produce a new certificate for a period of one year from the date of issue.

The application should refer to purchases of goods or services invoiced, during a period of not less than 3
months or not more than 1 calendar year. However, it may relate to a period of less than 3 months where this
period represents the remainder of a calendar year. It may also relate to invoices not covered by previous
applications and concerning transactions made during the calendar year in question.

Applications must be submitted within 6 months after the end of the calendar year in which the tax became
chargeable.

‘The total amount of VAT claimed must not be less than EUR 400 if the claim is for a period of at least 3

months but not more than a calendar year and not less than EUR 50 if the claim is for a period of less than 3
months, being all that remains of the calendar year.

10. Administrative obligations

10.1 Invoices

An Irish VAT invoice should include the following in order to constitute a valid VAT invoice:

+ name and address of the supplier;

+ VAT registration number of the supplier;

+ name and address of the person to whom the goods or services were supplied;

+ sequential number that uniquely identifies the invoice;

+ date of issue of the invoice;

+ — date of supply of the goods or services (or the date of prepayment/payment on account) if different than
the date of the invoice;

+ adescription of the goods or services supplied;

* quantity or volume of the goods supplied;

+ VAT exclusive price;

+ VAT rate(s) applicable:

+ VAT amount charged at each rate (except reverse charge supplies);

+ VAT number of customer in other EU Member State if invoice relates to reverse charge supply of goods
or services in relation to which the customer is Jiable to pay VAT;

+ VAT number of supplier and the customer in other EU Member in order to validly zero rate an invoice for
intra-Community supply purposes; and

+ indication that the invoice relates to an intra-Community supply of goods or reverse charge services (if
applicable).

10.1.1 Time limits

Invoices are generally issued at the time of supply of goods, but it is more difficult to identify a tax point for
ongoing supplies of services. Notwithstanding this, an invoice must be issued by the latest the 15" day of the
month following the month in which a tax point is triggered.

10.1.2 Currency

Invoices are to be issued in the currency of Ireland, which is EUR. But if an invoice is issued in a non-Euro
denominated currency, the invoice must contain the corresponding figures in EUR.

10.1.3 Conversion and exchange rate

There are rules to be adhered to for converting foreign currency amounts. The selling rate recorded by the
Central Bank of Ireland or the European Central Bank (ECB) at the time the VAT becomes due should be
used. It is possible to use other methods but this must be agreed in advance with Irish Revenue.

Ireland | 307

--- Page 124 ---
10.1.4 Simplified invoice

The Irish VAT legislation permits the issue of a simplified invoice, credit note, settlement voucher or debit
note provided certain conditions are met, namely, the amount of the invoice is less than EUR 100, or there are
certain practices (commercial, technical or administrative) in a particular business sector that make it difficult
to comply with the legislative invoicing requirements. A simplified invoice must however include certain
items at a minimum:

+ the date of issue of the invoice;

* the sequential number of the invoice:

+ the name, address and VAT registration number of the supplier;

+ the description of the supplies made; and

+ the VAT payable or the VAT exclusive consideration for the supply.

10.1.5 Self-billing
Self-billing may be used in Ireland. The conditions to enable self-billing include:

+ _ prior written agreement between the supplier and the customer;

+ the customer is VAT registered;

+ the details required to be on the invoice are satisfied by the customer;

+ agreed procedures are in place for the acceptance by the supplier of the validity of the invoice; and
* the invoice must state that the invoice has been “self-billed”.

‘The invoice is deemed to be issued by the supplier once the invoice is accepted by the supplier in accordance
with the agreed procedures,

10.1.6 Storage of invoices

Invoices and other books and records must be kept for a period of 6 years from the date of the latest
transaction to which the records, invoices or any other documents relate.

In relation to records for the acquisition or development of immovable goods, a taxable person must retain
records for the duration of its interest in the immovable goods plus a further period of 6 years.

For taxable persons that have exercised a waiver of exemption from VAT in respect of short-term lettings,
records must be retained for the duration of the waiver plus a further period of 6 years.

The requirement to keep records also applies to the following:

+ arecord relating to the exercising and terminating of a landlord’s option to tax;

+ acapital good record;

* arecord relating to a joint option to tax;

* adocument issucd in respect of a VATable surrender or assignment;

+ records in relation to a “qualifying conference” for the purposes of claiming an input VAT credit; and
+ — records in relation to transactions in investment gold.

However, these obligations do not apply if Irish Revenue have notified the person that retention is not
required,

Paper invoices must be stored in the original form, i.e. the invoices that have been issued in paper form must
be stored in paper form. Electronic invoices can be stored in paper form or in electronic form. Copies of
original paper invoices produced by microfilming or other copying processes are not acceptable.

Legislation does not require records to be stored at a particular location or jurisdiction. However, when Irish
Revenue wish to inspect records, the taxable person is obliged to make records available for inspection. Irish
Revenue personnel may travel abroad to inspect records that are stored overseas, although this would only
arise in exceptional circumstances.

308 | Ireland

--- Page 125 ---
10.1.7 Electronic invoicing

Electronic invoicing can only be used where there is prior agreement between the issuer and recipient. The
electronic system used must meet certain specifications. The system must be able to:

+ produce, retain and store electronic records and messages in such form and containing such particulars as
are required for VAT purposes, and make them available to Irish Revenue on request;

+ reproduce in paper or electronic format any electronic record or message required to be produced,
retained or stored; and

+ maintain electronic records in a manner that allows their retrieval by reference to the name of the person
who issues or receives the message or the date of the message or the unique identification number of the
message.

Parties to the invoice must be able to verify the authenticity of origin, integrity of content and a reliable audit
trail must exist between the parties.

Batch invoicing is also possible, whereby an accountable person issues a batch of invoices electronically to a
customer where the invoices record details that are common to al] the invoices only once per transmission

10.2 VAT compliance

10.2.1 VAT return period

‘Taxable periods in Ireland are generally bi-monthly - January / February, March / April, May / June, July /
August, September / October, November / December. However, alternative taxable periods may apply.

‘Accountable persons in a permanent VAT recoverable position may apply to file monthly VAT returns, but
approval is subject to review by the Office of the Collector General.

Trish Revenue may offer accountable persons the option to file annual VAT returns. If accepted, accountable
persons must make a monthly instalment of their annual VAT liability via direct debit. An accountable person
must ensure that the amount of the direct debit is sufficient so as not to result in a shortfall of VAT owing

at year end. If the shortfall is more than 20% of the overall VAT, then liability interest is payable from the
middle of the year.

VAT retums may be filed on a 4-monthly basis where the annual VAT liability for the taxable person is
between EUR 3,001 and EUR 14,400, Bi-annual / 6-monthly VAT retums may be filed if VAT payments are
less than EUR 3,000 annually.

The same taxable periods apply for domestic and foreign registered taxable persons.
Electronic filing of VAT returns is now mandatory for most accountable persons. This is done through the
Revenue-On-Line system (ROS).

10.2.2 Filing of a VAT return and payment

The VAT filing and payment deadlines in Ireland are the same for domestic accountable persons and non-
established taxable persons.

The filing deadline is the 19” day of the month following the end of the taxable period, but electronic returns

must be filed on ROS by the 23" day of the month following the end of the taxable period. E.g. an electronic

return for the January / February taxable period must be filed by 23 March. Electronic filing of VAT retarns is
mandatory. Where interest is applied for late payment, it applies from the 19% day, not the 23“ day.

VAT returns in Ireland require completion of the following information:

+ Box TL: VAT on sales = total VAT charged on sales in the period;
+ Box T2: VAT on purchases = total deductible VAT incurred;

Ireland | 309

--- Page 126 ---
+ Box T3: VAT payable = excess of VAT on sales over purchases (may be ‘nil”);

+ Box T4: VAT repayable = excess of VAT on purchases over sales (may be ‘nil’);

+ Box El: Intra-Community supplies of goods = the net total of goods sold to customers in other EU
Member States;

+ Box E2: Intra-Community acquisitions of goods = the net total of goods purchased from suppliers in other
EU Member States;

+ Box ESI: Intra-Community supply of services = the net total of services supplied to customers in other
EU Member States;

+ Box ES2: Intra-Community acquisition of services = the net total of services received from suppliers in
other EU Member States;

* Box PAI: Imports = the net value (plus carriage, freight and insurance) of imports from third countries.

10.2.3 European sales listing
European sales listings are referred to as VAT Information Exchange System (VIES) returns in Ireland.

In general, VIES returns for supplies of goods should be filed monthly, but accountable persons whose intra-
Community supplies of goods do not exceed the threshold of EUR 50,000 in any of the previous 4 quarters
may file VIES returns either on a quarterly basis, or annually in certain circumstances.

VIES returns for intra-Community supplies of services are generally filed quarterly, but accountable persons
may also file monthly VIES returns, if preferred. Annual returns may also be filed in certain circumstances.

VIES must be filed by the 23rd day of the month following the end of the reporting period and must be filed
online. Agents may file VIES returns for customers.

10.2.4 National recapitulative statement

‘There are no national recapitulative statements in Ireland. However, there is an annual statistical VAT return
to be filed which is referred to as the annual Return of Trading Details. This return is filed annually and
contains the net purchases and sales broken down by VAT rate.

10.2.5 Tax assessment period

The assessment period in Ireland is 4 years from the end of a taxable period.

10.3 Penalties

10.3.1 Default penalties for late registration
The statutory penalty which applies for failure to register for VAT within the required time period is EUR
4,000.

10.3.2 Default penalties for not filing a VAT return or a European sales listing on time

The statutory penalty for failure to file a VAT retum is EUR 4,000 per return.

The statutory penalty for failure to file a European sales listing (a VIES return) is EUR 4,000 per return.

10.3.3 Default penalties for not paying VAT on time to the VAT authorities

‘The late payment of VAT will be liable to interest of 0.0274% per day (approx. 10% per annum) that the VAT
liability remains unpaid in addition to a potential penalty of EUR 4,000 for failing to file the return on time.

10.3.4 Default penalties on VAT assessments when VAT is not reported correctly

In addition to the above penalties, the Irish Revenue can impose “tax-geared” penalties where an accountable
person has deliberately or carelessly filed incorrect VAT returns. The penalties may be mitigated where
different levels of disclosure are made by the accountable person and the level of cooperation received by
Irish Revenue. Disclosures may be prompted or unprompted, qualifying or non-qualifying. While penalties
may be mitigated, interest is not as it is statutorily imposed. A disclosure is treated as prompted when it is
made following receipt of notification of an Irish Revenue audit. A disclosure is unprompted if it is made
prior to receipt of an audit or investigation notification.

310 | Ireland

--- Page 127 ---
Under these provisions an accountable person can also be liable to penalties of EUR 3,000 where it has acted
carelessly or EUR 5,000 where it has acted deliberately in the production, sending or otherwise making use of
any incorrect invoices, registration number, credit note, debit note, receipt, account, voucher, bank statement,
estimate, book, document or record.

10.4 Intrastat

Intrastat returns are required to be filed on ROS on a monthly basis by accountable persons that exceed the
relevant thresholds.

10.4.1 Intrastat threshold

‘The arrivals threshold is EUR 750,000 per calendar year, The dispatch threshold is EUR 750,000 per
calendar year. A more detailed Intrastat retum is required where arrivals exceed EUR 5 million per annum or
dispatches exceed EUR 35 million per annum.

10.4.2 Intrastat data to be reported

Accountable persons must show the value of goods received from and dispatched to other EU Member States
in Boxes E] and E2 in their VAT return. However, if the above thresholds are exceeded, accountable persons
must file detailed monthly Intrastat returns. It is possible that accountable persons are only required to file a
detailed Intrastat return for arrivals or dispatches only, not both.

Information generally required:

+ EU Member State of origin/destination;

+ VAT number of the Intrastat trader and the declarant (if different to the Intrastat trader);
+ VAT number of the customer;

= nature of the transaction;

+ mode of transport:

+ 8-digit commodity code of the goods;

+ customs regime code;

* net weight of the goods: and

+ invoice value of the goods in EUR.

Returns must be filed by the 23 working day immediately following the end of the month to which
the return relates. A trader may appoint one or more agents to file Intrastat returns. VAT group remitters may
submit detailed monthly returns for the VAT group, although prior approval is required.

A trader who is registered for VAT in Ireland and registered in another Member State because of distance
sales to that Member State, must include the value of distance sales in the Intrastat Box E1 of the VAT3
return, Where a trader who is registered for VAT in Ireland makes distance sales to other Member States
but does not exceed the registration threshold in that Member State, Irish VAT must be accounted for on
the supply of goods. The VAT on the distance sales should be input in Box T1 (not in Box E1). A trader,
registered in another Member State who is also registered in Ireland because of distance sales to Ireland,
should include the value of distance sales in the Intrastat Box E2 of the VATS return.

A detailed Guide to VIES and Intrastat in Ireland can be found at hitps://tinyurl.com/48pjxkax

10.5 Additional Compliance Requirements

Ther are no additional requirements.

Italy

The European Union (EU) VAT Directive has been implemented in Italy through the DPR 26 October 1972,
no. 633 (hereafter referred to as “DPR 633/72” or “the VAT Law”). VAT is referred to in Italy as “Imposta sul
Valore Aggiunto” or “IVA”.

1. Taxable persons

1d Registration threshold & SME Scheme for domestic taxable persons

There is no registration threshold for established taxable persons. With respect to legal entities, VAT
registration is usually made through the registration of the entity with the Chamber of Commerce. The
following information must be provided for VAT registration to be performed directly by the Public Notary:

*  aregistration form;
* the by-laws; and
+ any authorizations required in connection with the business activities.

The same requirements apply to Italian branches of foreign companies.

With respect to individuals, a registration form must be filed electronically, by registered letter or directly
with the tax authorities. In case of individuals who also need to be registered at the Chamber of Commerce,
the VAT registration must be filed electronically.

Any change to the data reported in the form filed to register for VAT purposes must be communicated within
30 days from the date of the change.

12 Registration threshold & SME Scheme for foreign taxable persons

There is no registration threshold for non-established taxable persons. However, Italy introduced an
exemption for small and medium sized business per 1 January 2025.

In case of small enterprises established in another EU Member State, the SME scheme can be adopted
exclusively by a taxable natural person.

In particular, a taxable natural person established in another Member State of the European Union may apply

the relief regime in the territory of the Italian State if the below conditions are met:

+ in the preceding calendar year (N-1), the annual turnover achieved in the European Union did not exceed
EUR 100,000;

+ in the preceding calendar year (N-1), the annual turnover achieved in the territory of the Italian State did
not exceed EUR 85,000 (there are no sectoral thresholds in Italy);

+ in the current calendar year (N), up to the date of notification, the turnover in the European Union does
not exceed EUR 100,000;

+ it has submitted the prior notification to its Member State of establishment;

+ itis identified, for the purposes of applying the relief arrangements, by the identification number ‘EX’
exclusively in the Member State of establishment.

If the exemption cannot be applied, non-established taxable persons must apply for the VAT registration when
they perform taxable business transactions in Italy (i.¢., the application form should be filed no later than 30
days before the starting date of the activity).

Non-established taxable persons may opt for:

+ the appointment of a VAT representative, which is mandatory for non-EU taxable persons; or
+ — the direct identification, which is allowed only for taxable persons established in an EU Member State and

Italy | 313

--- Page 130 ---
non-BU persons who are established in countries with which the mutual assistance agreement has been
signed

In the case of a VAT representative, a mandate for the appointment of an Italian VAT representative must
be granted with a proxy. The date of the appointment must be certified. In order to do so, the signature on
the proxy must be legalized either by the Italian Consulate or by a local public notary. If the proxy is signed
before a public notary, the notary’s signature should be certified by an ‘apostille’ (according to The Hague
Convention) of the local competent authority.

If the proxy is not written in Italian, it must be translated and the translation must be sworn before an Italian
Consulate or an Italian Court. The VAT representative will then file the VAT registration form.

New fulfilments and requirements have been introduced for tax representatives and for non-resident entities in
the EU and the European Economic Area if they are already registered or intend to register for VIES in order
to carry out IC transactions.

Guarantee and requirements for VAT representatives

According to the ministerial decree dated 9 December 2024 published in the Oficial Gazette on 19 December

2024 (implementing the provisions provided for by Legislative Decree 12 February 2024, no. 13), VAT

representatives are required:

+ to certify to the tax Authority the presence of specific subjective requirements (such as no convictions for
financial crimes etc.); and

+ to provide a guarantee in favor of tax Authority,

‘The operational aspects to apply the provisions of the decree shall be provided by a measure not yet issued as
at 11 March 2025 (deadline for its issuance is 120 days from 19 December 2024).

Registration for VIES - Guarantee for non-resident entities in the EU and the European Economic
Area through VAT representative

The ministerial decree dated 4 December 2024 published in the Official Gazette on 13 December 2024
(implementing the provisions provided for by Legislative Decree 12 February 2024, no. 13) provides for the
tules regarding the guarantee to be filed by subjects not resident in the EU or in one of the States adhering to
the European Economic Area who intend to carry out intra~-Community transactions and operate through a tax
representative.

The operational aspects to apply the provisions of the decree shall be provided by a measure not yet issued as
at 11 March 2025 (deadline for its issuance is 120 days as from 13 December 2024).

In case of direct registration, the following documentation must be provided:

*  aregistration form;

+ an updated certificate issued by the foreign tax authorities stating that the taxable person is registered for
VAT purposes;

+ if legal entity, an updated certificate issued by the foreign Chamber of Commerce (or similar competent
authority) reporting the data of the entity and those relating to the legal representative(s) duly authorized
to sign and act on behalf of the company;

+ both certificates must be translated into Italian and signed by the translator;

* a copy of the identity document of the translator;

* acopy of an identity document of the legal representative; and

+ a statement signed by the legal representative, whereby the reasons for the request of VAT registration are
explained, including a description of any transactions eventually carried out before the VAT registration,
as well as the request of inclusion of the VAT registration number in the VIES system, should intra-
Community transactions be performed, with an indication of the expected amount of intra-Community
transactions. This statement must also be translated into Italian and signed by the translator.

In case of direct registration, the documentation must be filed exclusively with the Centro Operativo di
Pescara. The contact details are as follows:
